English for Work · Education & public service

Childcare and Early Education English

Build trusting family exchanges, support play and daily transitions, describe children's learning accurately, and coordinate inclusive care, health-record questions, and secure handovers.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Make yourself clear.

For Childcare workers, early educators, classroom assistants, and preschool support staff.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify conflicting arrival expectations with a parent while keeping the child's needs and responsible educator visible.
  • Use neutral prompts and comparative language to support shared investigation without choosing a winner for the children.
  • Offer bounded choices and a predictable next step without turning a transition into an open-ended negotiation.
  • Describe a peer disagreement neutrally and model a concrete turn-taking sequence without assigning an unobserved motive.
  • Share a specific learning observation warmly while rejecting unsupported developmental rankings and diagnoses.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All children, families, centers, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not childcare licensing, clinical advice, developmental diagnosis, or safeguarding certification. Follow current local law, supervision requirements, individual plans, and center procedures. Do not delay urgent safeguarding or emergency action for a routine query. Materials and activities require age-appropriate risk assessment. England and US sources provide distinct contexts, not interchangeable legal rules.

01Arrivals and family partnershipsAgreeing on the goodbye+

Your goal

Clarify conflicting arrival expectations with a parent while keeping the child's needs and responsible educator visible.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Agreeing on the goodbye

It is Mina's third morning at the center. Parent Daniel expected to stay for ten minutes, but the arrival note says a brief goodbye. Key educator Rosa is present and can clarify the settling-in plan with Daniel. Mina has a familiar cloth; using it must follow the center arrangements. No fixed departure time or guaranteed emotional response is established. Rosa must acknowledge the difference, avoid blaming Daniel or labeling Mina as difficult, and clarify the plan before giving inconsistent directions.

Daniel: Mina's parent / Rosa: Key educator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Daniel expect?

Answer and explanation

A. To stay for ten minutes

Daniel expected ten minutes, while the arrival note describes a brief goodbye.

2. Who can clarify the plan?

Answer and explanation

D. Rosa, Mina's key educator

Rosa is present and identified as the educator who can clarify the settling-in plan.

3. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. A fixed departure time and guaranteed emotional response

The case supplies conflicting expectations but not a completed agreement or guaranteed reaction.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Brief is not a universal number of minutes. Here it conflicts with Daniel expecting ten minutes, so the plan needs clarification. Do not convert either source into a finalized agreement before Rosa and Daniel resolve the practical meaning.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
settling-in plan
Agreed approach helping a child become familiar with a setting and its people.clarify the settling-in plan
key educator
Staff member with particular responsibility for supporting a child and family.speak with the key educator
arrival routine
Usual sequence when a child enters the setting.establish an arrival routine
family partnership
Working relationship in which families and educators share relevant knowledge.build a family partnership
handover
Transfer of relevant information and care responsibility between people.complete the morning handover
brief goodbye
Short departure exchange whose practical meaning needs agreement.agree on a brief goodbye
familiar object
Known item that may offer continuity or comfort within permitted arrangements.discuss a familiar object
comfort item
Object associated with reassurance, used under appropriate safety arrangements.check comfort-item arrangements
separation
Period or event in which a child and familiar caregiver are apart.support separation
reassurance
Supportive communication intended to reduce uncertainty or concern.offer realistic reassurance
attachment
Enduring emotional connection with a familiar caregiver or other significant person.support attachment relationships
responsive care
Care that notices and responds appropriately to the child's cues.provide responsive care
child cue
Observable signal of a child's interest, need, or emotional state.notice a child cue
predictability
Quality of an arrangement being understandable and reasonably consistent.build predictability
consistency
Alignment of actions or messages across people or occasions.maintain consistency
parent expectation
What a parent believes will happen in the arrangement.clarify the parent expectation
arrival note
Recorded information about the child's arrival or agreed approach.review the arrival note
agreed wording
Language adults have decided to use consistently.use agreed wording
transition support
Help with moving between people, settings, or activities.provide transition support
emotional response
Feelings or reactions expressed in a situation.observe the emotional response
individual need
Requirement specific to a particular child rather than an assumed group norm.respond to individual needs
update arrangement
Agreed method or time for sharing later information.clarify the update arrangement
contact preference
Preferred communication method where appropriate and authorized.confirm contact preferences
shared understanding
Agreement about what a plan or message means.establish shared understanding

A familiar cloth may support continuity, but its use depends on actual safety and center arrangements. Do not present a comfort item as automatically suitable for every age, activity, or sleep setting, and do not promise it will prevent distress.

C Notice the language

A partnership starts with listening

Parents and staff may attach different meanings to a brief goodbye. Acknowledge the expectation without promising an arrangement before it is clarified. The child should hear calm, consistent language rather than adults correcting one another sharply at the door.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the expectation
You expected to stay for ten minutes.
Read the note neutrally
The arrival note says a brief goodbye.
Name the difference
Those expectations need to be clarified together.
Identify the responsible adult
I am Mina's key educator, and we can clarify the plan.
Keep the child central
Let us keep our messages to Mina calm and consistent.
Avoid blame
We do not need to decide who is at fault to clarify this.
Reject a guarantee gently
I cannot promise exactly how Mina will respond.
Notice the familiar item
Mina has brought her familiar cloth.
Check the arrangement
Its use needs to follow our center arrangements.
Do not invent a deadline
We have not yet agreed on a departure time here.
Invite useful information
Please tell me what you expected the goodbye to involve.
Preserve the plan
We should record the clarified arrangement through our process.
Keep support concrete
Mina needs to know which adult is receiving her.
Avoid a fixed label
One arrival does not define Mina as a difficult child.
Clarify later contact
Any update arrangement should be agreed rather than assumed.
Close together
Let us confirm the plan and use the same message with Mina.
6 language and precision notes
You expected
Recognizes the parent's understanding without declaring it the final plan.
The note says
Attributes the other message rather than using it as an accusation.
Together
Frames clarification as cooperation instead of correction of the parent.
Exactly how
Limits promises about the child's feelings.
Not yet agreed
Prevents a possible arrangement from becoming a confirmed instruction.
Same message
Connects adult coordination with the child's experience.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most useful?

Answer and explanation

B. You expected ten minutes; the note says brief goodbye. Let us clarify the plan together.

The opening preserves both sources and invites a practical agreement without blame or a false guarantee.

2. What should be said about the cloth?

Answer and explanation

D. Its use follows the center's actual arrangements.

Arrival comfort, sleep safety, and the child's response are separate questions; familiarity does not settle all three.

3. Why should adults coordinate their wording?

Answer and explanation

D. To give Mina a calm, consistent message about the clarified plan

Consistent communication supports a clearer transition without making the child resolve an adult disagreement.

4. Which statement overreaches?

Answer and explanation

A. Mina will definitely be calm as soon as Daniel leaves.

The facts do not establish or guarantee Mina's future emotional response.

E Conversations

Agreeing on the goodbye

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Daniel: Mina's parent / Rosa: Key educator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • brief goodbye
  • settling-in plan
  • consistency
  • key educator
  • comfort item
  • emotional response
  • update arrangement
  • arrival routine
  • family partnership
  • shared understanding
  1. Daniel
    Rosa, I thought I could stay for ten minutes this morning, but the note says a brief goodbye. I do not want Mina hearing two different instructions.
  2. Rosa
    Thank you for raising that. We need to clarify the 1 together. The note and your expectation do not yet give us one agreed approach.
  3. Daniel
    It is only her third morning, and I had prepared her for me staying a little while. I am worried about changing the message at the door.
  4. Rosa
    I am her 2, so let us work through the difference calmly. We should not make Mina choose between what you expected and what the note appears to say.
  5. Daniel
    When staff say brief, do they mean a particular number of minutes? I cannot tell whether ten minutes fits that word or contradicts it.
  6. Rosa
    A 3 needs a clear meaning in our arrangement. I should not treat that word as though we have already agreed on an exact departure time.
  7. Daniel
    Yesterday I was told not to rush. This morning the note sounds different. I need to know what to do, and Mina is listening to us.
  8. Rosa
    This is about 4, not blame. We can acknowledge what you understood and clarify the plan without labeling your choice as the cause of a reaction we have not observed.
  9. Daniel
    She has brought her familiar cloth. It helps her recognize something from home, but I do not know the center arrangements for using it.
  10. Rosa
    We should check the 5 arrangements. Its familiarity is useful information, but I will not assume it can be used in every activity or setting.
  11. Daniel
    Could you promise that she will be settled after I leave? I know that may be difficult to answer, but it would make leaving easier.
  12. Rosa
    I can offer honest support, not a guaranteed 6. We will need to notice how Mina responds and follow the agreed approach rather than promise a particular feeling.
  13. Daniel
    Could you be the person she comes to after our goodbye? She knows you now, and I would like to tell her who will be with her.
  14. Rosa
    Agreed. A consistent 7 should make the next step understandable. Let us clarify the practical handover and use the same message with her.
  15. Daniel
    Will tomorrow's staff see what we agree? I do not want to prepare Mina for one routine and be given a different instruction when we arrive.
  16. Rosa
    Yes. We should preserve the 8 through our process. A clear record will help colleagues continue the arrangement without inventing a new version.
  17. Daniel
    And how will I hear how she gets on? I keep checking my phone, but I do not know when I should expect an update.
  18. Rosa
    We can clarify the 9 as part of the conversation. We should say what contact is genuinely agreed rather than leave you waiting for an assumed promise.
  19. Daniel
    Thank you. My main concern is that Mina gets one calm message and that I understand the plan before being told to leave.
  20. Rosa
    That is why 10 matters here. Let us resolve the expectation, confirm the handover, and keep the message to Mina clear and respectful.

Answers and explanations
  1. settling-in plan Turn 2. The settling-in plan is the arrangement being clarified, not a universal rule that automatically resolves the two different expectations.
  2. key educator Turn 4. The key educator is Rosa, the present staff member identified to clarify the plan with Daniel and support Mina.
  3. brief goodbye Turn 6. A brief goodbye needs a shared practical meaning here; the word alone does not specify a universal number of minutes.
  4. family partnership Turn 8. Family partnership treats Daniel's information as part of the clarification rather than assigning blame for an unresolved arrival arrangement.
  5. comfort item Turn 10. The comfort item is the familiar cloth; its use depends on actual center and safety arrangements, not an automatic permission for every situation.
  6. emotional response Turn 12. An emotional response cannot be promised in advance; Rosa can support Mina without guaranteeing a specific reaction to separation.
  7. arrival routine Turn 14. The arrival routine is the understandable sequence adults clarify for Mina, not a promise that every morning will feel identical.
  8. shared understanding Turn 16. Shared understanding means the practical agreement is clear to the adults, rather than relying on the unresolved word brief.
  9. update arrangement Turn 18. An update arrangement specifies actual later communication; it must be agreed rather than assumed or offered as an unsupported timing guarantee.
  10. consistency Turn 20. Consistency aligns the adults' messages after clarification, helping Mina receive one coherent plan without an invented departure deadline.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daniel: Rosa, I thought I could stay for ten minutes this morning, but the note says a brief goodbye. I do not want Mina hearing two different instructions.
  2. Rosa: Thank you for raising that. We need to clarify the settling-in plan together. The note and your expectation do not yet give us one agreed approach.
  3. Daniel: It is only her third morning, and I had prepared her for me staying a little while. I am worried about changing the message at the door.
  4. Rosa: I am her key educator, so let us work through the difference calmly. We should not make Mina choose between what you expected and what the note appears to say.
  5. Daniel: When staff say brief, do they mean a particular number of minutes? I cannot tell whether ten minutes fits that word or contradicts it.
  6. Rosa: A brief goodbye needs a clear meaning in our arrangement. I should not treat that word as though we have already agreed on an exact departure time.
  7. Daniel: Yesterday I was told not to rush. This morning the note sounds different. I need to know what to do, and Mina is listening to us.
  8. Rosa: This is about family partnership, not blame. We can acknowledge what you understood and clarify the plan without labeling your choice as the cause of a reaction we have not observed.
  9. Daniel: She has brought her familiar cloth. It helps her recognize something from home, but I do not know the center arrangements for using it.
  10. Rosa: We should check the comfort item arrangements. Its familiarity is useful information, but I will not assume it can be used in every activity or setting.
  11. Daniel: Could you promise that she will be settled after I leave? I know that may be difficult to answer, but it would make leaving easier.
  12. Rosa: I can offer honest support, not a guaranteed emotional response. We will need to notice how Mina responds and follow the agreed approach rather than promise a particular feeling.
  13. Daniel: Could you be the person she comes to after our goodbye? She knows you now, and I would like to tell her who will be with her.
  14. Rosa: Agreed. A consistent arrival routine should make the next step understandable. Let us clarify the practical handover and use the same message with her.
  15. Daniel: Will tomorrow's staff see what we agree? I do not want to prepare Mina for one routine and be given a different instruction when we arrive.
  16. Rosa: Yes. We should preserve the shared understanding through our process. A clear record will help colleagues continue the arrangement without inventing a new version.
  17. Daniel: And how will I hear how she gets on? I keep checking my phone, but I do not know when I should expect an update.
  18. Rosa: We can clarify the update arrangement as part of the conversation. We should say what contact is genuinely agreed rather than leave you waiting for an assumed promise.
  19. Daniel: Thank you. My main concern is that Mina gets one calm message and that I understand the plan before being told to leave.
  20. Rosa: That is why consistency matters here. Let us resolve the expectation, confirm the handover, and keep the message to Mina clear and respectful.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Mina's third morning

Fictional arrival conversation using the lesson case.

  1. Parent: I expected ten minutes together during this settling-in period. Why does the note say brief goodbye?
  2. Educator: I can see the difference. Rosa, Mina's key educator, is here to clarify today's settling-in plan.
  3. Parent: Mina brought her familiar cloth. Yesterday she wanted it while looking around the room.
  4. Educator: Thanks. I'll include that comfort item in the family handover, without assuming Mina wants it all morning.
  5. Parent: Could you promise me she won't cry once I leave? That is what worries me.
  6. Educator: I can't promise her feelings, but Rosa can explain how we'll support her and communicate with you.
  7. Parent: Please ask Rosa before I say goodbye, so Mina doesn't hear two different plans.
  8. Educator: Yes. Mina, your parent and I are checking today's plan with Rosa together.
  9. Parent: I'd like consistent goodbye words, even if today's arrangement needs clarification.
  10. Educator: Let's agree those words with Rosa now, then we can all give Mina one clear message.
The morning update is from home

Fictional arrival handover; parent reports a disrupted night without requesting medical advice.

  1. Parent: Owen woke earlier than usual and seems quieter to me this morning. I wanted you to know.
  2. Educator: I'll identify that as your home observation, not a conclusion about the day.
  3. Parent: Please don't assume he is upset with the classroom. He hasn't told me that.
  4. Educator: I won't assign a reason. Is there anything else you want his key educator to hear?
  5. Parent: He asked for the book corner when we arrived. That's his familiar place here.
  6. Educator: I'll tell her about that request and the earlier waking, keeping the two facts separate.
  7. Parent: Could you share actual observations later, rather than just saying he was fine?
  8. Educator: Yes. We can share concrete observations in the family handover, within our usual contact arrangements.
  9. Parent: Concrete observations would help me understand the morning without guessing from his quiet arrival.
  10. Educator: I'll give his key educator the update now and mention his request for the book corner.
A name pronounced correctly

Fictional first-week family conversation; educator checks the child's name.

  1. Educator: I want to say Leila's name correctly. Could you model how your family pronounces it?
  2. Parent: Of course. The first sound is longer than the way you said it yesterday.
  3. Educator: Thank you. Let me try it again, and please correct me if I miss it.
  4. Parent: That's closer. Leila notices when people rush the name, though she may not correct adults herself.
  5. Educator: I'll take time with it and ask colleagues to listen to your pronunciation too.
  6. Parent: Could you also use the same name, rather than shortening it without asking her?
  7. Educator: Yes. We can use Leila and check her preference instead of choosing a nickname.
  8. Parent: She has been quiet at arrival, but she told me she likes hearing her name properly.
  9. Educator: That's useful to know. I'll greet her directly and give her time to answer.
  10. Parent: Thank you. I'll help her hear the same pronunciation when we introduce ourselves to other staff.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Agreeing on the goodbye
  1. Read Daniel and Rosa's checked dialogue. Contrast expected ten minutes with the note's brief goodbye without blaming the parent.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the handover and update questions, keeping every unconfirmed arrangement conditional.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify another arrival mismatch

Parent Ellis expects a five-minute stay. The note says immediate handover. Key educator Noor is present. No revised agreement has been made.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Ellis: "I expected a ___ stay."

Answer and explanation

D. five-minute

The parent expectation is a five-minute stay, not an agreed revision.

2. Noor: "The note says ___."

Answer and explanation

A. immediate handover

Immediate handover is the wording in the existing note.

3. Ellis: "The key educator available is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Noor

Noor is the present educator identified to clarify the arrangement.

4. Noor: "The revised agreement is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. confirmed

No revised arrangement has been agreed in the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Arrivals and family partnerships
Original fictional case: It is Mina's third morning at the center. Parent Daniel expected to stay for ten minutes, but the arrival note says a brief goodbye. Key educator Rosa is present and can clarify the settling-in plan with Daniel. Mina has a familiar cloth; using it must follow the center arrangements. No fixed departure time or guaranteed emotional response is established. Rosa must acknowledge the difference, avoid blaming Daniel or labeling Mina as difficult, and clarify the plan before giving inconsistent directions.
Language workshop: Agreeing on the goodbye
Communication goal: Clarify conflicting arrival expectations with a parent while keeping the child's needs and responsible educator visible.
Language guidance: Brief is not a universal number of minutes. Here it conflicts with Daniel expecting ten minutes, so the plan needs clarification. Do not convert either source into a finalized agreement before Rosa and Daniel resolve the practical meaning.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You expected to stay for ten minutes. / The arrival note says a brief goodbye. / Those expectations need to be clarified together. / I am Mina's key educator, and we can clarify the plan. / Let us keep our messages to Mina calm and consistent. / We do not need to decide who is at fault to clarify this. / I cannot promise exactly how Mina will respond. / Mina has brought her familiar cloth. / Its use needs to follow our center arrangements. / We have not yet agreed on a departure time here. / Please tell me what you expected the goodbye to involve. / We should record the clarified arrangement through our process. / Mina needs to know which adult is receiving her. / One arrival does not define Mina as a difficult child. / Any update arrangement should be agreed rather than assumed. / Let us confirm the plan and use the same message with Mina.
Vocabulary: settling-in plan: Agreed approach helping a child become familiar with a setting and its people. / key educator: Staff member with particular responsibility for supporting a child and family. / arrival routine: Usual sequence when a child enters the setting. / family partnership: Working relationship in which families and educators share relevant knowledge. / handover: Transfer of relevant information and care responsibility between people. / brief goodbye: Short departure exchange whose practical meaning needs agreement. / familiar object: Known item that may offer continuity or comfort within permitted arrangements. / comfort item: Object associated with reassurance, used under appropriate safety arrangements. / separation: Period or event in which a child and familiar caregiver are apart. / reassurance: Supportive communication intended to reduce uncertainty or concern. / attachment: Enduring emotional connection with a familiar caregiver or other significant person. / responsive care: Care that notices and responds appropriately to the child's cues. / child cue: Observable signal of a child's interest, need, or emotional state. / predictability: Quality of an arrangement being understandable and reasonably consistent. / consistency: Alignment of actions or messages across people or occasions. / parent expectation: What a parent believes will happen in the arrangement. / arrival note: Recorded information about the child's arrival or agreed approach. / agreed wording: Language adults have decided to use consistently. / transition support: Help with moving between people, settings, or activities. / emotional response: Feelings or reactions expressed in a situation. / individual need: Requirement specific to a particular child rather than an assumed group norm. / update arrangement: Agreed method or time for sharing later information. / contact preference: Preferred communication method where appropriate and authorized. / shared understanding: Agreement about what a plan or message means.
Speaking task: Read Daniel and Rosa's checked dialogue. Contrast expected ten minutes with the note's brief goodbye without blaming the parent. Switch roles. Repeat the handover and update questions, keeping every unconfirmed arrangement conditional. Complete the five-minute/immediate-handover transfer, check the key, then read it without inventing a revised agreement.
Partner: Daniel: Mina's parent / Rosa: Key educator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Parent Ellis expects a five-minute stay. The note says immediate handover. Key educator Noor is present. No revised agreement has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daniel: And how will I hear how she gets on? I keep checking my phone, but I do not know when I should expect an update.
Rosa: We can clarify the update arrangement as part of the conversation. We should say what contact is genuinely agreed rather than leave you waiting for an assumed promise.
Daniel: Thank you. My main concern is that Mina gets one calm message and that I understand the plan before being told to leave.
Rosa: That is why consistency matters here. Let us resolve the expectation, confirm the handover, and keep the message to Mina clear and respectful.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

REFERENCE
Course: Childcare and Early Education English
Professional audience: Childcare workers, early educators, classroom assistants, and preschool support staff
Scope: All children, families, centers, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not childcare licensing, clinical advice, developmental diagnosis, or safeguarding certification. Follow current local law, supervision requirements, individual plans, and center procedures. Do not delay urgent safeguarding or emergency action for a routine query. Materials and activities require age-appropriate risk assessment. England and US sources provide distinct contexts, not interchangeable legal rules.
Lesson 01: Arrivals and family partnerships
Original fictional case: It is Mina's third morning at the center. Parent Daniel expected to stay for ten minutes, but the arrival note says a brief goodbye. Key educator Rosa is present and can clarify the settling-in plan with Daniel. Mina has a familiar cloth; using it must follow the center arrangements. No fixed departure time or guaranteed emotional response is established. Rosa must acknowledge the difference, avoid blaming Daniel or labeling Mina as difficult, and clarify the plan before giving inconsistent directions.
Language workshop: Agreeing on the goodbye
Communication goal: Clarify conflicting arrival expectations with a parent while keeping the child's needs and responsible educator visible.
Language guidance: Brief is not a universal number of minutes. Here it conflicts with Daniel expecting ten minutes, so the plan needs clarification. Do not convert either source into a finalized agreement before Rosa and Daniel resolve the practical meaning.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You expected to stay for ten minutes. / The arrival note says a brief goodbye. / Those expectations need to be clarified together. / I am Mina's key educator, and we can clarify the plan. / Let us keep our messages to Mina calm and consistent. / We do not need to decide who is at fault to clarify this. / I cannot promise exactly how Mina will respond. / Mina has brought her familiar cloth. / Its use needs to follow our center arrangements. / We have not yet agreed on a departure time here. / Please tell me what you expected the goodbye to involve. / We should record the clarified arrangement through our process. / Mina needs to know which adult is receiving her. / One arrival does not define Mina as a difficult child. / Any update arrangement should be agreed rather than assumed. / Let us confirm the plan and use the same message with Mina.
Vocabulary: settling-in plan: Agreed approach helping a child become familiar with a setting and its people. / key educator: Staff member with particular responsibility for supporting a child and family. / arrival routine: Usual sequence when a child enters the setting. / family partnership: Working relationship in which families and educators share relevant knowledge. / handover: Transfer of relevant information and care responsibility between people. / brief goodbye: Short departure exchange whose practical meaning needs agreement. / familiar object: Known item that may offer continuity or comfort within permitted arrangements. / comfort item: Object associated with reassurance, used under appropriate safety arrangements. / separation: Period or event in which a child and familiar caregiver are apart. / reassurance: Supportive communication intended to reduce uncertainty or concern. / attachment: Enduring emotional connection with a familiar caregiver or other significant person. / responsive care: Care that notices and responds appropriately to the child's cues. / child cue: Observable signal of a child's interest, need, or emotional state. / predictability: Quality of an arrangement being understandable and reasonably consistent. / consistency: Alignment of actions or messages across people or occasions. / parent expectation: What a parent believes will happen in the arrangement. / arrival note: Recorded information about the child's arrival or agreed approach. / agreed wording: Language adults have decided to use consistently. / transition support: Help with moving between people, settings, or activities. / emotional response: Feelings or reactions expressed in a situation. / individual need: Requirement specific to a particular child rather than an assumed group norm. / update arrangement: Agreed method or time for sharing later information. / contact preference: Preferred communication method where appropriate and authorized. / shared understanding: Agreement about what a plan or message means.
Speaking task: Read Daniel and Rosa's checked dialogue. Contrast expected ten minutes with the note's brief goodbye without blaming the parent. Switch roles. Repeat the handover and update questions, keeping every unconfirmed arrangement conditional. Complete the five-minute/immediate-handover transfer, check the key, then read it without inventing a revised agreement.
Partner: Daniel: Mina's parent / Rosa: Key educator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Parent Ellis expects a five-minute stay. The note says immediate handover. Key educator Noor is present. No revised agreement has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daniel: And how will I hear how she gets on? I keep checking my phone, but I do not know when I should expect an update.
Rosa: We can clarify the update arrangement as part of the conversation. We should say what contact is genuinely agreed rather than leave you waiting for an assumed promise.
Daniel: Thank you. My main concern is that Mina gets one calm message and that I understand the plan before being told to leave.
Rosa: That is why consistency matters here. Let us resolve the expectation, confirm the handover, and keep the message to Mina clear and respectful.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Play-based learning and useful promptsTwo bridge ideas+

Your goal

Use neutral prompts and comparative language to support shared investigation without choosing a winner for the children.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Two bridge ideas

During fictional block play, Eva and Jo each want their bridge design used. Eva wants a wider bridge; Jo wants a taller opening underneath. Educators Maya and Ben discuss language that will invite both explanations and support trying two designs with the available blocks. No winner has been chosen, and no test outcome is supplied. The task is to model useful child-facing phrases inside an adult planning conversation. A prompt should leave room for the child idea without quietly supplying the answer or making the activity a competition.

Maya: Early-years educator / Ben: Colleague supporting block play

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Eva want?

Answer and explanation

D. A wider bridge

Eva's stated priority is the width of the bridge.

2. What does Jo want?

Answer and explanation

D. A taller opening underneath

Jo's stated priority is the height of the opening underneath.

3. What can the educators suggest?

Answer and explanation

D. Try two designs using the available blocks.

The brief allows two designs with available blocks but supplies no winner or outcome.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Wider and taller refer to different dimensions. A bridge can be wide without having a tall opening. Preserve each child design intention before suggesting a comparison, and do not change taller into better or wider into stronger without evidence.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
play-based learning
Learning developed through purposeful opportunities for play and exploration.support play-based learning
child-led idea
Proposal originating with the child rather than prescribed by an adult.follow a child-led idea
adult scaffolding
Temporary support that helps a learner do more while retaining meaningful participation.provide adult scaffolding
open prompt
Invitation allowing a range of relevant responses rather than supplying one answer.use an open prompt
leading question
Question that steers the listener toward an implied preferred answer.avoid a leading question
wait time
Deliberate pause allowing a child time to process and respond.allow wait time
sustained shared thinking
Extended joint exploration of an idea or problem through interaction.support sustained shared thinking
modeling
Demonstrating language or action that can support learning.use language modeling
recasting
Restating a child's message with an adjusted grammatical form while preserving the intended meaning.use a gentle recast
expansion
Adding useful language to a child's expressed idea while preserving its meaning.offer a language expansion
comparative language
Words used to describe relative qualities such as wider or taller.model comparative language
spatial language
Words describing position, direction, distance, or arrangement.introduce spatial language
width
Measurement across an object from side to side.compare bridge width
height
Vertical measurement from a defined lower point to a higher point.compare opening height
span
Distance bridged between supports.compare the span
support
Part holding up or stabilizing a structure.position the supports
stability
Ability of a structure to remain in position under relevant conditions.explore stability
clearance
Space available between an object and a surrounding or overhead surface.check the clearance
design intention
What the maker wants a design to do or achieve.clarify the design intention
prediction
Statement about what someone expects to happen before trying it.make a prediction
trial
Attempt used to explore how something works.compare two trials
revision
Change made to a design or idea after further thinking or testing.revise the design
collaborative play
Play involving coordination or shared activity with others.support collaborative play
specific feedback
Response naming an observed action or feature rather than general praise alone.give specific feedback

An open prompt invites the child idea; a leading question suggests the adult answer. Comparing structures is different from ranking children. Useful feedback names an observed feature, while a prediction remains an expectation until the relevant trial has occurred.

C Notice the language

Curiosity can be precise

An educator can give language support without taking ownership of the design. Name width and height clearly, allow both ideas to be expressed, and avoid praise that makes one child the winner before the structures have been explored.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Invite Eva's idea
Eva, show us what you mean by a wider bridge.
Invite Jo's idea
Jo, show us where you want the taller opening.
Name both priorities
Eva is exploring width, and Jo is exploring opening height.
Avoid a winner
We have two ideas to try, not a winner already chosen.
Offer a feasible trial
We can try two designs with these available blocks.
Model comparison
This opening is taller; this bridge is wider.
Ask without supplying
What do you want this part of the bridge to do?
Allow processing
I will pause so each child has time to respond.
Preserve the idea
I can expand the language without replacing the child's intention.
Separate prediction and result
That is what you expect; now we can see what happens.
Give specific feedback
You moved the supports farther apart.
Use spatial language
The opening is underneath the bridge.
Keep the activity shared
Let us hear both explanations before choosing what to try.
Avoid an unsupported claim
A wider design is not automatically the stronger one.
Invite a revision
You can change the design after seeing what happens.
Close with exploration
We can compare the two designs without ranking the children.
6 language and precision notes
Show us what you mean
Allows gesture and demonstration alongside words.
Where
Directs attention to the relevant part without prescribing the design.
Two ideas to try
Frames the activity as exploration rather than a contest.
What do you want
Keeps the intention with the child.
Expect versus happens
Separates prediction from observation.
Ranking the children
Distinguishes comparing structures from labeling children's ability.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which prompt preserves Jo's idea?

Answer and explanation

D. Show us where you want the taller opening.

The prompt preserves opening height as Jo's priority without supplying a design or ranking.

2. Which sentence is a leading question?

Answer and explanation

B. Wouldn't it be better to use my wider design?

The question embeds the adult preferred answer instead of inviting the child's own explanation.

3. Which comparison is factual in principle?

Answer and explanation

A. This bridge is wider, while that opening is taller.

Width and opening height can be described separately without ranking children or inventing results.

4. What is the proposed next action?

Answer and explanation

C. Try two designs using available blocks.

The stated opportunity is a feasible comparison, not a guaranteed result or predetermined competition.

E Conversations

Two bridge ideas

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Maya: Early-years educator / Ben: Colleague supporting block play

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • expansion
  • wait time
  • leading question
  • specific feedback
  • collaborative play
  • spatial language
  • open prompt
  • Comparative language
  • trial
  • design intention
  1. Ben
    Eva and Jo both want their bridge idea used. Eva wants it wider, while Jo keeps showing me a taller opening underneath. I am tempted to choose one.
  2. Maya
    Let us clarify each 1 first. We can ask Eva to show the width and Jo to show the opening, without deciding whose idea is better.
  3. Ben
    I could say, "Eva, show us what you mean by wider." That lets her point or demonstrate rather than requiring a long explanation.
  4. Maya
    Yes. That is an 2. It supports her idea without putting our answer into her mouth or turning the exchange into a vocabulary test.
  5. Ben
    For Jo, I could ask where the opening should be taller. I should not translate his request into another version of Eva's idea.
  6. Maya
    Exactly. Use 3 such as underneath while keeping the idea his. Height of an opening and width of a bridge are different features.
  7. Ben
    I caught myself saying, "Wouldn't a wider bridge be better?" That was really my idea in question form. I need to give Jo's opening a fair hearing too.
  8. Maya
    That is a 4. It could make Jo feel that the decision is already made. We can invite explanations without hinting which child should agree with us.
  9. Ben
    We have blocks available for trying two designs. I could suggest that practical option instead of making the children compete for one approved plan.
  10. Maya
    A second 5 gives us something to compare. We should not announce the result in advance or promise that a particular structure will work.
  11. Ben
    I keep saying good job, but it does not give them much to work with. What could I say about the supports they are moving?
  12. Maya
    Offer 6 such as, "You moved the supports farther apart." That names an action and leaves room for the child to explain what they intended.
  13. Ben
    I can also model wider and taller while pointing to the appropriate dimension. I should not use taller as another word for better.
  14. Maya
    Right. 7 helps describe the structures. It should not quietly become a ranking of the children or an unsupported claim about strength.
  15. Ben
    If Eva says, "More space here," I might say, "You want more space across the bridge," while checking that I have kept her meaning.
  16. Maya
    That is a useful 8. We are adding language around her idea, not correcting her into a different design or requiring her to repeat our exact sentence.
  17. Ben
    I tend to fill the silence. Eva may be about to point when I jump in with another question. I will try leaving that space.
  18. Maya
    Allow 9. Watch their responses, including gestures, and keep the exploration moving at a pace that allows both to participate.
  19. Ben
    Then our plan is to hear both intentions, offer two available-block trials, and describe what happens without choosing a winner beforehand.
  20. Maya
    Yes. That supports 10. We can compare features, invite a later revision, and keep the children's ideas at the center of the conversation.

Answers and explanations
  1. design intention Turn 2. Design intention identifies what each child wants the structure to achieve, so the two different priorities can be heard before choosing a trial.
  2. open prompt Turn 4. An open prompt invites Eva's meaning through words or demonstration instead of supplying the adult's preferred design.
  3. spatial language Turn 6. Spatial language identifies the opening underneath the bridge and helps preserve the location relevant to Jo's proposal.
  4. leading question Turn 8. A leading question contains an implied preferred answer, here favoring the wider design before the children's ideas are explored.
  5. trial Turn 10. A trial is an attempt to explore a design; proposing two trials does not establish either outcome or a winner.
  6. specific feedback Turn 12. Specific feedback names an observed action or feature, giving useful language without replacing the child's idea with general praise or ranking.
  7. Comparative language Turn 14. Comparative language describes relative qualities such as width and height, not an automatic judgment that one child or design is better.
  8. expansion Turn 16. Expansion adds language to the child's expressed meaning while preserving the intention rather than substituting an adult idea.
  9. wait time Turn 18. Wait time leaves room for processing and response; answering immediately for the children would remove that opportunity.
  10. collaborative play Turn 20. Collaborative play involves shared exploration and coordination, which the two-design comparison supports without a predetermined winner.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Eva and Jo both want their bridge idea used. Eva wants it wider, while Jo keeps showing me a taller opening underneath. I am tempted to choose one.
  2. Maya: Let us clarify each design intention first. We can ask Eva to show the width and Jo to show the opening, without deciding whose idea is better.
  3. Ben: I could say, "Eva, show us what you mean by wider." That lets her point or demonstrate rather than requiring a long explanation.
  4. Maya: Yes. That is an open prompt. It supports her idea without putting our answer into her mouth or turning the exchange into a vocabulary test.
  5. Ben: For Jo, I could ask where the opening should be taller. I should not translate his request into another version of Eva's idea.
  6. Maya: Exactly. Use spatial language such as underneath while keeping the idea his. Height of an opening and width of a bridge are different features.
  7. Ben: I caught myself saying, "Wouldn't a wider bridge be better?" That was really my idea in question form. I need to give Jo's opening a fair hearing too.
  8. Maya: That is a leading question. It could make Jo feel that the decision is already made. We can invite explanations without hinting which child should agree with us.
  9. Ben: We have blocks available for trying two designs. I could suggest that practical option instead of making the children compete for one approved plan.
  10. Maya: A second trial gives us something to compare. We should not announce the result in advance or promise that a particular structure will work.
  11. Ben: I keep saying good job, but it does not give them much to work with. What could I say about the supports they are moving?
  12. Maya: Offer specific feedback such as, "You moved the supports farther apart." That names an action and leaves room for the child to explain what they intended.
  13. Ben: I can also model wider and taller while pointing to the appropriate dimension. I should not use taller as another word for better.
  14. Maya: Right. Comparative language helps describe the structures. It should not quietly become a ranking of the children or an unsupported claim about strength.
  15. Ben: If Eva says, "More space here," I might say, "You want more space across the bridge," while checking that I have kept her meaning.
  16. Maya: That is a useful expansion. We are adding language around her idea, not correcting her into a different design or requiring her to repeat our exact sentence.
  17. Ben: I tend to fill the silence. Eva may be about to point when I jump in with another question. I will try leaving that space.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two bridges worth trying

Fictional educator and child spokesperson discuss the lesson's two-design case.

  1. Educator: Eva wants a wide bridge, and you want a taller opening. What would yours let you do?
  2. Child: I want the big wooden truck to go underneath, not just the little one.
  3. Educator: That explains the height. What did Eva say she wanted the wider top to hold?
  4. Child: She wants two trucks side by side. We thought only one design could be right.
  5. Educator: We have enough blocks to try both. How could you decide which to build first?
  6. Child: We could start with the tall one, then use the other blocks for the wide one.
  7. Educator: That gives each idea a turn. What will you test on yours?
  8. Child: Whether the big truck fits underneath without moving the bridge when I push it through.
  9. Educator: Tell Eva that plan and ask what she wants to test on her wider bridge.
  10. Child: I'll ask her about the two trucks. Then we can compare what each bridge does.
A shop in the play corner

Fictional guided-play exchange; child designs a pretend shop.

  1. Educator: You arranged the baskets like a shop. What would you like your customers to find?
  2. Child: I have pretend fruit in this basket and empty boxes in the other one.
  3. Educator: How will someone know where to bring the things they want to buy?
  4. Child: I haven't made that part yet. They could put everything on this small table.
  5. Educator: What could you use to show that the table is the place to pay?
  6. Child: Maybe a picture of a coin. I can draw one on the little sign.
  7. Educator: That is your idea for the sign. What might you say when a customer arrives?
  8. Child: I could say welcome to my shop, then ask which basket they want to look in.
  9. Educator: Would you like me to be a customer while you try your greeting?
  10. Child: Yes. Please ask for an empty box first, because I want to practice showing that basket.
When a prompt became an instruction

Fictional colleague discussion about scaffolding in child-initiated play.

  1. Educator: I noticed you suggested adding a roof, and the child put down the bridge they were making.
  2. Colleague: I intended to help, but I may have changed the child's project instead.
  3. Educator: What was the child trying to do before you offered that new direction?
  4. Colleague: They wanted to connect two sides. The roof was my idea, not theirs.
  5. Educator: Could we return to their interest with an open-ended question about joining the sides?
  6. Colleague: Yes. I could ask what they want to connect, then wait for their explanation.
  7. Educator: That would offer scaffolding without deciding the whole structure. They may have a different solution.
  8. Colleague: I also asked three questions quickly. They didn't get time to answer the first one.
  9. Educator: Let's offer one prompt in this child-initiated play and wait to see whether more support is wanted.
  10. Colleague: I'll return to the bridge idea and ask what help they want with the connection.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Play-based learning and useful prompts
Original fictional case: During fictional block play, Eva and Jo each want their bridge design used. Eva wants a wider bridge; Jo wants a taller opening underneath. Educators Maya and Ben discuss language that will invite both explanations and support trying two designs with the available blocks. No winner has been chosen, and no test outcome is supplied. The task is to model useful child-facing phrases inside an adult planning conversation. A prompt should leave room for the child idea without quietly supplying the answer or making the activity a competition.
Language workshop: Two bridge ideas
Communication goal: Use neutral prompts and comparative language to support shared investigation without choosing a winner for the children.
Language guidance: Wider and taller refer to different dimensions. A bridge can be wide without having a tall opening. Preserve each child design intention before suggesting a comparison, and do not change taller into better or wider into stronger without evidence.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Eva, show us what you mean by a wider bridge. / Jo, show us where you want the taller opening. / Eva is exploring width, and Jo is exploring opening height. / We have two ideas to try, not a winner already chosen. / We can try two designs with these available blocks. / This opening is taller; this bridge is wider. / What do you want this part of the bridge to do? / I will pause so each child has time to respond. / I can expand the language without replacing the child's intention. / That is what you expect; now we can see what happens. / You moved the supports farther apart. / The opening is underneath the bridge. / Let us hear both explanations before choosing what to try. / A wider design is not automatically the stronger one. / You can change the design after seeing what happens. / We can compare the two designs without ranking the children.
Vocabulary: play-based learning: Learning developed through purposeful opportunities for play and exploration. / child-led idea: Proposal originating with the child rather than prescribed by an adult. / adult scaffolding: Temporary support that helps a learner do more while retaining meaningful participation. / open prompt: Invitation allowing a range of relevant responses rather than supplying one answer. / leading question: Question that steers the listener toward an implied preferred answer. / wait time: Deliberate pause allowing a child time to process and respond. / sustained shared thinking: Extended joint exploration of an idea or problem through interaction. / modeling: Demonstrating language or action that can support learning. / recasting: Restating a child's message with an adjusted grammatical form while preserving the intended meaning. / expansion: Adding useful language to a child's expressed idea while preserving its meaning. / comparative language: Words used to describe relative qualities such as wider or taller. / spatial language: Words describing position, direction, distance, or arrangement. / width: Measurement across an object from side to side. / height: Vertical measurement from a defined lower point to a higher point. / span: Distance bridged between supports. / support: Part holding up or stabilizing a structure. / stability: Ability of a structure to remain in position under relevant conditions. / clearance: Space available between an object and a surrounding or overhead surface. / design intention: What the maker wants a design to do or achieve. / prediction: Statement about what someone expects to happen before trying it. / trial: Attempt used to explore how something works. / revision: Change made to a design or idea after further thinking or testing. / collaborative play: Play involving coordination or shared activity with others. / specific feedback: Response naming an observed action or feature rather than general praise alone.
Speaking task: Read Maya and Ben's corrected dialogue. Use your voice to distinguish a wider bridge from a taller opening. Switch roles. Read the exact child-facing prompts, pausing after each prompt instead of supplying a child's answer. Complete the tower comparison, check the key, then read it aloud without changing wider, taller, or the two proposed trials.
Partner: Maya: Early-years educator / Ben: Colleague supporting block play
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ari wants a wider tower base. Bea wants a taller tower. Available blocks allow two trials. No winner or result is established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: I tend to fill the silence. Eva may be about to point when I jump in with another question. I will try leaving that space.
Maya: Allow wait time. Watch their responses, including gestures, and keep the exploration moving at a pace that allows both to participate.
Ben: Then our plan is to hear both intentions, offer two available-block trials, and describe what happens without choosing a winner beforehand.
Maya: Yes. That supports collaborative play. We can compare features, invite a later revision, and keep the children's ideas at the center of the conversation.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
