Vocabulary: observation record: Account of what was noticed in a defined context. / anecdotal note: Brief factual account of a meaningful event or behavior. / verbatim quotation: Exact words reproduced without changing their wording. / context: Circumstances in which an event or response occurred. / work sample: Example of a child's work used as one source of information. / learning story: Narrative account connecting observed learning with its context and meaning. / classification: Grouping items according to shared features. / sorting criterion: Feature used to decide which items belong together. / attribute: Observable feature such as color, shape, or size. / one-to-one correspondence: Matching one item with one other item or one count word. / cardinality: Understanding that the final count word represents the number in a set. / fine-motor skill: Coordinated use of small muscles, often in hand and finger activity. / developmental domain: Area of development such as language, physical, or social development. / developmental milestone: Skill or behavior associated with development, interpreted with appropriate variation and context. / formative assessment: Information used during learning to guide support and next steps. / summative assessment: Assessment of achievement at a defined endpoint. / developmental screening: Structured process identifying whether further developmental evaluation may be needed. / diagnostic assessment: Qualified evaluation used to establish a diagnosis or detailed explanation. / comparison group: Relevant group used as a basis for interpreting comparative results. / norm-referenced measure: Assessment interpreted in relation to an appropriate reference population. / criterion-referenced measure: Assessment interpreted against specified criteria rather than a peer ranking alone. / reliability: Consistency of a measurement or assessment under relevant conditions. / validity: Support for the intended interpretation and use of assessment information. / strength-based description: Account that recognizes capabilities without exaggerating or ignoring support needs.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Inclusive participation and home languagesPointing is a response+

Your goal

Explain an allowed participation choice while distinguishing access, engagement, and evidence of spoken language.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Pointing is a response

A small-group story activity allows picture choices or spoken answers under Noor's current inclusion plan. Noor prefers pointing today. Colleague Ben describes that as nonparticipation. Educator Amina must explain that pointing is an allowed response without claiming it supplies the same evidence as a spoken sentence. No reason for Noor's preference, diagnosis, or home-language profile is supplied. The team can use inclusive language, record the actual response format, and keep future support connected to the plan rather than requiring speech as an unannounced condition of belonging.

Amina: Early-years educator / Ben: Colleague observing the group

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which response formats does the plan allow?

Answer and explanation

A. Picture choices or spoken answers

Noor's current plan explicitly allows both picture choices and spoken answers.

2. What does Noor prefer today?

Answer and explanation

B. Pointing

The stated preference is pointing, not an inferred reason or diagnosis.

3. What should the team avoid claiming?

Answer and explanation

D. That pointing supplies identical evidence to a spoken sentence

Different formats can support participation while providing different evidence of expressive spoken language.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A permitted pointing response can be participation without being a spoken sentence. Record what Noor selected and how the response was made. Do not convert pointing into either no participation or evidence of a spoken-language performance that did not occur.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
inclusion plan
Agreed support arrangements for meaningful access and participation.follow the inclusion plan
participation
Taking part in an activity through an appropriate available form.recognize participation
engagement
Attention or involvement in an activity, described through relevant evidence.observe engagement
response mode
Way a person answers or communicates in an activity.record the response mode
picture choice
Selection among images used to express a response.offer picture choices
pointing response
Indication made by pointing rather than speaking.acknowledge a pointing response
spoken response
Answer communicated through speech.record a spoken response
expressive language
Language used to convey meaning to others.support expressive language
receptive language
Understanding of language received through listening, reading, or other input.distinguish receptive language
communication access
Opportunity and support needed to receive and convey meaning.provide communication access
visual support
Image or visual arrangement that helps understanding or expression.use visual supports
gesture
Movement used to communicate meaning.notice a gesture
augmentative communication
Methods that support existing speech or communication.use appropriate augmentative communication
alternative communication
Methods used instead of speech for some or all communication.support alternative communication
AAC
Augmentative and alternative communication: tools and strategies supporting communication.follow the AAC plan
home language
Language used in the child's home or family life.value the home language
dual language learner
Child learning two or more languages, including English alongside a home language.support a dual language learner
language difference
Variation connected with languages used, distinct from an assumed disorder.distinguish a language difference
language delay
Language skills developing more slowly than expected, assessed in context rather than inferred from one response or multilingualism.refer a language-delay concern
multilingual repertoire
Languages and communication resources a person can draw on.recognize a multilingual repertoire
family language information
Details families share about language use and communication.gather family language information
access barrier
Feature that prevents or limits meaningful participation.identify an access barrier
equitable opportunity
Fair access that may require different supports rather than identical treatment.provide equitable opportunity
evidence limit
Boundary on what an observation supports.state the evidence limit

Noor choosing to point today does not establish a diagnosis, reluctance, home language, or general language ability. Home-language information should come from appropriate sources and family partnership, not inference from a name or a single classroom response.

C Notice the language

Participation is broader than one response format

A quiet response may be easy to overlook in a busy group. Identify what the child actually did and whether it fits the activity plan. Respect a permitted communication choice without pretending that pointing and speaking demonstrate exactly the same language skills.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Refer to the plan
Noor's current plan allows picture choices or spoken answers.
Name today's choice
Noor prefers pointing today.
Recognize participation
Pointing is an allowed response in this activity.
State the observation
Noor selected a picture by pointing.
Keep evidence precise
No spoken sentence was recorded in that response.
Avoid an all-or-nothing label
A nonspeaking response is not automatically nonparticipation.
Separate the aims
Access to the story and evidence of spoken language are related but distinct.
Reject a guessed diagnosis
This response alone does not establish a language delay.
Avoid a language assumption
We do not know Noor's home-language profile from this observation.
Keep support planned
Any change in support should follow the appropriate planning process.
Use the available options
We can make both permitted response formats accessible.
Respect today's preference
We should not remove the allowed choice because speech is more noticeable.
Value family information
Family language information should retain its source and context.
Avoid false equivalence
The two response modes do not provide identical evidence.
Name the barrier
Requiring speech without changing the plan would remove an allowed route.
Close accurately
Noor participated by pointing; that is the response we should record.
6 language and precision notes
Current plan
Anchors the response choice to the actual arrangement.
Today
Limits the statement to the observed preference rather than a permanent trait.
Allowed response
Connects the action to participation criteria.
No spoken sentence
States the evidence limit without denying engagement.
Related but distinct
Prevents access and assessment from being treated as the same question.
From this observation
Blocks unsupported conclusions about language background or diagnosis.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which note is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Noor participated by selecting a picture through pointing; no spoken sentence was recorded.

The note preserves the permitted participation and the actual response mode without inventing speech or diagnosis.

2. Which action conflicts with the current plan?

Answer and explanation

B. Require spoken answers as the only way to count participation.

The plan allows picture choices, so an unannounced speech-only rule removes a permitted route.

3. What can be inferred about the home language?

Answer and explanation

D. Nothing specific from the supplied pointing preference alone

The case supplies no home-language profile, and a single response does not establish one.

4. Which distinction matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Participation can be valid while evidence of spoken-language production remains limited.

Inclusive access and precise assessment can coexist without false equivalence between response modes.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Pointing is a response

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Amina: Early-years educator / Ben: Colleague observing the group

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  • inclusion plan
  • access barrier

  1. Ben
    Noor did not give a spoken answer in the story group. I was going to mark that as not participating, but you seem to have recorded a response.
  2. Amina
    The 1 allows picture choices or spoken answers. Noor preferred pointing today and selected a picture, which is an allowed response in this activity.
  3. Ben
    I saw the pointing, but I was listening for a sentence. I may have treated the response I expected as the only response that counted.
  4. Amina
    That confuses 2 with one particular format. We should recognize the actual response while staying precise about what it demonstrates.
  5. Ben
    Does that mean we should describe pointing as exactly the same evidence as a spoken answer? I do not want our notes to lose the difference.
  6. Amina
    No. The 3 matters. We can write that Noor selected a picture by pointing and that no spoken sentence was recorded in that response.
  7. Ben
    I will change my note from no response to selected a picture by pointing. That is what I saw; I did not hear a sentence.
  8. Amina
    Exactly. The 4 stays visible. A meaningful contribution does not need to be exaggerated into evidence of a different kind of performance.
  9. Ben
    Could the preference mean a language delay, or tell us which language Noor uses at home? I have heard colleagues make those assumptions.
  10. Amina
    Neither follows from this moment. A 5 or concern needs appropriate information, not an inference from one pointing response or from the child's name.
  11. Ben
    So we should not record a particular home language unless we actually have that information through the proper family and record process.
  12. Amina
    Correct. 6 should be gathered respectfully and attributed accurately. We should not create a language profile to explain an observation we have not investigated.
  13. Ben
    If I insist on a spoken sentence before Noor can count as taking part, I would be removing one of the routes the plan currently allows.
  14. Amina
    That would create an 7. We can support the activity through its available options rather than change the participation rule without the appropriate process.
  15. Ben
    We still need to notice how children use language over time. Recognizing pointing should not mean we stop describing the language we actually hear.
  16. Amina
    Agreed. Evidence of 8 should identify the actual form and context. Precise observation and inclusive participation are compatible, not opposing goals.
  17. Ben
    Before the next group, let us put the picture choices where Noor can reach them. They are not much help if they stay behind my chair.
  18. Amina
    Yes. The 9 needs to be accessible through the planned arrangement. We should check the environment rather than interpret a missing opportunity as a child failure.
  19. Ben
    I will change my note to describe the pointing response and its limits. I will not label it nonparticipation or infer a diagnosis or home language.
  20. Amina
    That supports 10. Noor used an allowed option today, and we can keep both the participation and the response difference clear.

Answers and explanations
  1. inclusion plan Turn 2. The inclusion plan explicitly permits picture choices or spoken answers, so speech is not the only allowed participation route.
  2. participation Turn 4. Participation means taking part through an appropriate available form; here a permitted pointing response can count without spoken language.
  3. response mode Turn 6. The response mode records how Noor answered, preserving pointing as distinct from a spoken sentence.
  4. evidence limit Turn 8. The evidence limit prevents an allowed pointing response from being treated as proof of spoken-language production that did not occur.
  5. language difference Turn 10. A language difference and a developmental concern require appropriate contextual information; Noor's single pointing preference establishes neither.
  6. Family language information Turn 12. Family language information comes from appropriate sources and retains its context; it cannot be invented from a response preference or name.
  7. access barrier Turn 14. An access barrier would arise from excluding the picture-choice route that Noor's plan currently permits for this activity.
  8. expressive language Turn 16. Expressive language concerns conveying meaning; the record must identify the actual form rather than invent a spoken sentence or erase other communication.
  9. visual support Turn 18. Visual support must be usable within the activity; merely listing picture choices in a plan does not make them practically accessible.
  10. equitable opportunity Turn 20. Equitable opportunity can involve different supports while preserving accurate evidence about how each child participated.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Noor did not give a spoken answer in the story group. I was going to mark that as not participating, but you seem to have recorded a response.
  2. Amina: The inclusion plan allows picture choices or spoken answers. Noor preferred pointing today and selected a picture, which is an allowed response in this activity.
  3. Ben: I saw the pointing, but I was listening for a sentence. I may have treated the response I expected as the only response that counted.
  4. Amina: That confuses participation with one particular format. We should recognize the actual response while staying precise about what it demonstrates.
  5. Ben: Does that mean we should describe pointing as exactly the same evidence as a spoken answer? I do not want our notes to lose the difference.
  6. Amina: No. The response mode matters. We can write that Noor selected a picture by pointing and that no spoken sentence was recorded in that response.
  7. Ben: I will change my note from no response to selected a picture by pointing. That is what I saw; I did not hear a sentence.
  8. Amina: Exactly. The evidence limit stays visible. A meaningful contribution does not need to be exaggerated into evidence of a different kind of performance.
  9. Ben: Could the preference mean a language delay, or tell us which language Noor uses at home? I have heard colleagues make those assumptions.
  10. Amina: Neither follows from this moment. A language difference or concern needs appropriate information, not an inference from one pointing response or from the child's name.
  11. Ben: So we should not record a particular home language unless we actually have that information through the proper family and record process.
  12. Amina: Correct. Family language information should be gathered respectfully and attributed accurately. We should not create a language profile to explain an observation we have not investigated.
  13. Ben: If I insist on a spoken sentence before Noor can count as taking part, I would be removing one of the routes the plan currently allows.
  14. Amina: That would create an access barrier. We can support the activity through its available options rather than change the participation rule without the appropriate process.
  15. Ben: We still need to notice how children use language over time. Recognizing pointing should not mean we stop describing the language we actually hear.
  16. Amina: Agreed. Evidence of expressive language should identify the actual form and context. Precise observation and inclusive participation are compatible, not opposing goals.
  17. Ben: Before the next group, let us put the picture choices where Noor can reach them. They are not much help if they stay behind my chair.
  18. Amina: Yes. The visual support needs to be accessible through the planned arrangement. We should check the environment rather than interpret a missing opportunity as a child failure.
  19. Ben: I will change my note to describe the pointing response and its limits. I will not label it nonparticipation or infer a diagnosis or home language.
  20. Amina: That supports equitable opportunity. Noor used an allowed option today, and we can keep both the participation and the response difference clear.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A picture choice is participation

Fictional colleague exchange using the lesson case.

  1. Educator: Noor chose the picture response allowed in the inclusion plan. Why did the note say no participation?
  2. Colleague: I expected a spoken answer and overlooked the picture choice as a response.
  3. Educator: Both formats let Noor indicate a story choice, though they don't show the same spoken-English evidence.
  4. Colleague: Then I should record the choice rather than treat the absence of speech as no answer.
  5. Educator: Exactly. Noor pointed to the picture of the bus when asked which part to revisit.
  6. Colleague: Can I say that proves Noor can explain the choice aloud in English?
  7. Educator: Not from pointing alone. We can recognize participation without adding an unobserved spoken explanation.
  8. Colleague: I'll correct the observation and keep the response format clear for the next educator.
  9. Educator: Please do. Today's preference was pointing; the plan also allows speech when Noor chooses it.
  10. Colleague: That helps me distinguish access to the activity from the particular language evidence I hoped to observe.
A greeting from home

Fictional family shares a familiar greeting; educator checks rather than assuming translation.

  1. Educator: Your child used a greeting I didn't recognize. Would you like to tell me what it means?
  2. Parent: It's the greeting we use at home in the morning. I can say it slowly for you.
  3. Educator: Thank you. May I try it back so you can check my pronunciation?
  4. Parent: Yes, and I can explain when we normally use it, because context matters too.
  5. Educator: Thank you. I won't assume each home-language word has one exact English substitute.
  6. Parent: Could you let my child use it without insisting on an English version immediately?
  7. Educator: We can welcome that greeting and model our English greeting alongside it, without treating yours as wrong.
  8. Parent: That would help. My child enjoys showing people words but dislikes being put on display.
  9. Educator: I'll offer an invitation, not ask for a performance in front of everyone.
  10. Parent: Thank you. We can share a few familiar words privately as part of our family communication.
A quieter meeting option

Fictional family conference planning; phone or private in-person meeting available, neither chosen yet.

  1. Educator: We can arrange a private in-person conversation or a telephone meeting. Which format works better for you?
  2. Parent: The telephone is easier for travel, but I sometimes need to see the portfolio examples.
  3. Educator: An in-person meeting lets us view the examples together; a call avoids the journey.
  4. Parent: Could we check whether the examples can be shared through the center's permitted family channel beforehand?
  5. Educator: I'll check before promising that. Your communication preference matters to this choice.
  6. Parent: Please don't assume telephone means I want a shorter discussion or less detail about my child.
  7. Educator: I won't. The format and the content are different questions; we can plan the same topics.
  8. Parent: Once you confirm the sharing arrangement, I can decide which format is practical.
  9. Educator: I'll check with the lead before booking a format without your agreement.
  10. Parent: Then we'll choose based on the actual options, not just which method is easiest for staff.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Pointing is a response
  1. Read Amina and Ben's corrected dialogue. Contrast selected a picture with no spoken sentence, without calling the child unresponsive.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the exchange about making the picture choices physically accessible under the current plan.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Record another permitted response mode

Ari's current story plan permits a spoken answer or a picture selection. Ari selects a picture today. No spoken answer or home-language information is supplied.

Answer and explanation

D. picture

2. Colleague: "A spoken answer was not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. recorded

No spoken answer is supplied in this observation.

3. Educator: "The choice was allowed by the current ___."

Answer and explanation

D. plan

The plan permits either the spoken or picture response.

4. Colleague: "The home-language profile remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unspecified

No home-language information is supplied by this particular observation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 06: Inclusive participation and home languages
Original fictional case: A small-group story activity allows picture choices or spoken answers under Noor's current inclusion plan. Noor prefers pointing today. Colleague Ben describes that as nonparticipation. Educator Amina must explain that pointing is an allowed response without claiming it supplies the same evidence as a spoken sentence. No reason for Noor's preference, diagnosis, or home-language profile is supplied. The team can use inclusive language, record the actual response format, and keep future support connected to the plan rather than requiring speech as an unannounced condition of belonging.
Language workshop: Pointing is a response
Communication goal: Explain an allowed participation choice while distinguishing access, engagement, and evidence of spoken language.
Language guidance: A permitted pointing response can be participation without being a spoken sentence. Record what Noor selected and how the response was made. Do not convert pointing into either no participation or evidence of a spoken-language performance that did not occur.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Noor's current plan allows picture choices or spoken answers. / Noor prefers pointing today. / Pointing is an allowed response in this activity. / Noor selected a picture by pointing. / No spoken sentence was recorded in that response. / A nonspeaking response is not automatically nonparticipation. / Access to the story and evidence of spoken language are related but distinct. / This response alone does not establish a language delay. / We do not know Noor's home-language profile from this observation. / Any change in support should follow the appropriate planning process. / We can make both permitted response formats accessible. / We should not remove the allowed choice because speech is more noticeable. / Family language information should retain its source and context. / The two response modes do not provide identical evidence. / Requiring speech without changing the plan would remove an allowed route. / Noor participated by pointing; that is the response we should record.

Attention or involvement in an activity, described through relevant evidence. / response mode: Way a person answers or communicates in an activity. / picture choice: Selection among images used to express a response. / pointing response: Indication made by pointing rather than speaking. / spoken response: Answer communicated through speech. / expressive language: Language used to convey meaning to others. / receptive language: Understanding of language received through listening, reading, or other input. / communication access: Opportunity and support needed to receive and convey meaning. / visual support: Image or visual arrangement that helps understanding or expression. / gesture: Movement used to communicate meaning. / augmentative communication: Methods that support existing speech or communication. / alternative communication: Methods used instead of speech for some or all communication. / AAC: Augmentative and alternative communication: tools and strategies supporting communication. / home language: Language used in the child's home or family life. / dual language learner: Child learning two or more languages, including English alongside a home language. / language difference: Variation connected with languages used, distinct from an assumed disorder. / language delay: Language skills developing more slowly than expected, assessed in context rather than inferred from one response or multilingualism. / multilingual repertoire: Languages and communication resources a person can draw on. / family language information: Details families share about language use and communication. / access barrier: Feature that prevents or limits meaningful participation. / equitable opportunity: Fair access that may require different supports rather than identical treatment. / evidence limit: Boundary on what an observation supports.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Health records and factual incident handoversA reported allergy-plan change+

Your goal

Receive a parent health-plan update and make an accountable document handoff without inventing a care decision.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A reported allergy-plan change

Theo's parent, Morgan, tells educator Rosa that the allergy plan may have changed. The center still holds the earlier plan, and no revised document is available in this conversation. Health coordinator Amira is present and accepts the document query. No food decision, exposure, symptoms, or emergency is part of this case. Rosa must take the report seriously, preserve the difference between a reported change and a verified revision, and connect Morgan with Amira promptly. The exercise does not authorize changing treatment, relaxing controls, or ignoring a reported safety concern.

Morgan: Theo's parent / Rosa: Early-years educator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Morgan report?

Answer and explanation

C. The allergy plan may have changed.

The report concerns a possible plan change, not a completed revision or an exposure.

2. Which status is established when Amira accepts the document query?

Answer and explanation

D. The question has a named receiving owner.

Amira accepts ownership of the query; verification, version replacement, and staff communication are not yet established.

3. What remains unavailable?

Answer and explanation

C. The revised document

No revised document is supplied, so its contents cannot be treated as verified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

May have changed identifies a reported uncertainty. It is neither proof that the earlier plan remains suitable nor permission to invent a replacement. Escalate promptly through the real health process so the responsible people address the discrepancy and any immediate implications.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
allergy action plan
Individualized document specifying management of a known allergy under appropriate guidance.verify the allergy action plan
healthcare plan
Agreed information and arrangements for a person's relevant health needs.review the healthcare plan
reported change
Update described by a source but not necessarily verified.record a reported change
verified revision
Updated version confirmed through the appropriate process.obtain a verified revision
current version
Document version established as applicable for use.confirm the current version
superseded version
Earlier document replaced through the appropriate process.identify a superseded version
document control
Process for managing document versions, approval, access, and changes.follow document control
health coordinator
Person responsible for coordinating specified health-information processes.contact the health coordinator
parent report
Information attributed to a child's parent or carer.preserve the parent report
source document
Original or authoritative record supporting an instruction or fact.request the source document
care instruction
Direction governing an aspect of care from the appropriate source.clarify a care instruction
emergency action plan
Defined arrangements for responding to an emergency.follow the emergency action plan
allergen
Substance capable of triggering an allergic reaction in a susceptible person.identify the relevant allergen
food intolerance
Adverse food response distinct from an immune-mediated food allergy.distinguish food intolerance
allergic reaction
Immune response to an allergen that may require appropriate medical action.recognize an allergic reaction
anaphylaxis
Serious allergic reaction requiring immediate emergency response.follow anaphylaxis procedures
exposure
Contact with a substance or situation relevant to the concern.record a reported exposure
cross-contact
Unintended transfer of an allergen from one food or surface to another.prevent allergen cross-contact
medication authorization
Required permission or authority for medicine-related action under applicable rules.verify medication authorization
training record
Documentation of completed relevant staff training.check the training record
staff briefing
Sharing of relevant information with staff through an appropriate process.document the staff briefing
incident record
Factual account of a relevant event, actions, and outcomes.complete an incident record
handoff acknowledgment
Confirmation that the receiving person accepted the query or information.obtain handoff acknowledgment
unresolved query
Question that still requires an appropriate answer or action.track the unresolved query

This case has no food decision, exposure, symptoms, or emergency. Do not create one in the record. In actual care, urgent or emergency action must follow the applicable plan and procedures without waiting for a routine document exchange.

C Notice the language

Take an update seriously without guessing its content

A missing revised document does not make a parent report unimportant. Acknowledge the concern and give it a receiving owner. At the same time, do not invent the revision, alter a treatment instruction, or announce that every relevant staff member has been updated before that happens.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the report
Thank you for telling me the plan may have changed.
State the held record
We still hold the earlier plan.
Name the missing document
The revised document is not available here.
Preserve uncertainty
I will record this as a reported possible change.
Name the receiving owner
Amira has accepted the document query.
Make the next step immediate
Let us connect you with Amira now.
Avoid inventing the revision
I cannot state the new instructions without the appropriate verification.
Do not dismiss the report
The missing document does not mean we should ignore what you have told us.
Keep decisions separate
This conversation does not establish a food or treatment decision.
Protect sensitive information
We should handle the health information through the appropriate private process.
Check actual receipt
Amira's acceptance gives the query an owner.
Avoid false completion
The plan has not been confirmed as revised yet.
Keep staff updates factual
Do not record a staff briefing before it has occurred.
Preserve version history
The document process should show which version is applicable.
Keep emergency priorities
Actual urgent concerns must follow the appropriate emergency or health route.
Close accurately
Possible change reported, earlier plan held, revision unavailable, query accepted by Amira.
6 language and precision notes
May have changed
Retains the parent's uncertainty without dismissing the report.
Still hold
Describes the available document, not a judgment that it remains suitable.
Has accepted
Identifies a confirmed receiving person.
Now
Keeps a relevant safety-information query from drifting into an unspecified future task.
Not confirmed yet
Prevents a received question from being labeled a completed plan update.
Before it has occurred
Keeps documentation tied to actual communication events.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Parent reports a possible plan change; earlier plan held; revision unavailable; Amira accepts the query.

The handoff preserves the report, document gap, and receiving owner without inventing a care event or completion.

2. What does Amira accepting the query establish?

Answer and explanation

A. The document question has a receiving owner.

Acceptance assigns the query but does not complete all subsequent verification and communication.

3. Which statement is unsafe as a general inference?

Answer and explanation

D. No revised paper means the reported concern can be ignored.

A missing document does not remove the need to address a reported health concern through the appropriate process.

4. What should not be added to the incident record for this case?

Answer and explanation

A. An invented exposure, symptom, or treatment

The case explicitly supplies no exposure, symptoms, or treatment event, so recording one would be false.

A reported allergy-plan change

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Theo's parent / Rosa: Early-years educator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • handoff acknowledgment
  • incident record
  • health coordinator
  • verified revision
  • reported change
  • staff briefing
  • allergy action plan
  • parent report
  • unresolved query
  • document control
  1. Morgan
    Rosa, I need to tell you that Theo's allergy plan may have changed. I do not have the revised document with me, but I do not want this overlooked.
  2. Rosa
    Thank you for telling me. I will record the 1 and connect you with health coordinator Amira, who is here and has accepted the document query.
  3. Morgan
    Does the fact that you still have the old document mean staff will simply assume nothing has changed? That is what I am concerned about.
  4. Rosa
    We need to address the 2 through our actual health process. Holding an earlier plan does not justify ignoring your report or guessing the new instructions.
  5. Morgan
    I need to check the new paperwork myself. Please do not take what I am saying as a complete set of replacement instructions.
  6. Rosa
    I will preserve that 3 accurately. The record should not turn may have changed into a completed update or a treatment instruction you did not provide.
  7. Morgan
    Who will check which version should apply? I need to know this has reached someone responsible, rather than become a general note no one owns.
  8. Rosa
    Amira is the 4 receiving the query. Let us connect you with her now so the discrepancy and its practical implications are handled through the appropriate process.
  9. Morgan
    I am calling about the paperwork, not to report a reaction today. I do not want anyone to think I have described an incident that did not happen.
  10. Rosa
    I will not add an 5 that invents such an event. The information we have is your possible-plan-change report and the missing revised document.
  11. Morgan
    I am worried another member of staff will see a note saying updated and assume you already have the new document. You do not have it yet.
  12. Rosa
    That is why 6 matters. The earlier plan is held, the revised document is unavailable here, and the applicable version needs the appropriate confirmation.
  13. Morgan
    I can try to get the document through the approved route. Until then, I would rather the team ask Amira than piece together instructions from my memory.
  14. Rosa
    Agreed. A 7 cannot be created by guessing. We must use the real source and authorization process, including any required communication to the people who need it.
  15. Morgan
    Once I speak with Amira, will she confirm what still needs doing? I do not want to leave believing the plan has been checked if it has not.
  16. Rosa
    No. The 8 confirms ownership. It does not establish a completed verification, new care decision, or finished staff briefing.
  17. Morgan
    That distinction helps. Please keep the information private while making sure it reaches the appropriate staff through the actual process.
  18. Rosa
    Yes. Any 9 must be appropriate and recorded accurately. We should not claim that everyone has received new instructions before that has happened.
  19. Morgan
    Then my report is taken seriously, but no one is pretending I supplied a complete new plan today. I will speak with Amira now.
  20. Rosa
    Correct. The 10 question is with Amira. We will preserve the source, the missing revision, and the actual next actions without inventing a food or treatment decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. reported change Turn 2. The reported change preserves Morgan's information as a possible update, not as a verified revision or an irrelevant comment.
  2. unresolved query Turn 4. The unresolved query concerns the possible change and missing revision; holding an earlier document does not settle its implications.
  3. parent report Turn 6. The parent report must retain Morgan's wording and uncertainty rather than become a confirmed new clinical instruction.
  4. health coordinator Turn 8. The health coordinator is the named receiving owner who is present and has accepted this document question.
  5. incident record Turn 10. An incident record must contain actual reported or observed events; this conversation does not supply an exposure, reaction, or treatment.
  6. document control Turn 12. Document control preserves the status and version of the available plan and the unresolved revision, rather than silently substituting an assumed update.
  7. verified revision Turn 14. A verified revision requires the appropriate source and process; rewriting from memory would not establish an authorized update.
  8. handoff acknowledgment Turn 16. Handoff acknowledgment confirms Amira accepted the question, not that the revised plan or staff communication is complete.
  9. staff briefing Turn 18. A staff briefing must actually occur through the appropriate process before being recorded as completed; it is not implied by the first handoff.
  10. allergy action plan Turn 20. The allergy action plan requires appropriate verification of its applicable instructions; this conversation establishes the query and handoff, not a replacement plan.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A possible change to Theo's plan

Fictional document handover using the lesson case; no food, exposure, symptom, or emergency decision.

  1. Educator: Theo's parent reports a possible change to the allergy care plan, but we have no revised document.
  2. Health coordinator: What did the parent provide, and which version is currently held by the center?
  3. Educator: Only a verbal report. The earlier plan is still the document in our record.
  4. Health coordinator: I accept the plan query. I'll clarify the information with the family and the relevant qualified professional.
  5. Educator: A colleague asked whether that report means today's food arrangement is different.
  6. Health coordinator: I haven't confirmed any change. Don't infer a food decision from this incomplete document query.
  7. Educator: I'll make the distinction clear: possible change reported, revised plan not received, and you are reviewing it.
  8. Health coordinator: Please also identify the parent as the source, rather than present the message as a clinical instruction.
  9. Educator: I will. No symptoms or exposure have been reported in this scenario.
  10. Health coordinator: Thank you. I'll handle the clarification while the record accurately shows what remains unconfirmed.
What the educator actually saw

Fictional resolved playground incident documentation; the lead has handled care and family response separately.

  1. Educator: I'm completing the factual handover about the earlier incident, after you handled the care response.
  2. Lead educator: Tell me what you directly witnessed, and keep the child's later account separate.
  3. Educator: My witness account is seeing the child seated beside the step; the child reported stumbling.
  4. Lead educator: Then your observation is sitting beside the step; the stumble comes from the child's account.
  5. Educator: I didn't see the earlier movement, so I haven't stated how it happened.
  6. Lead educator: Please keep that distinction in the incident record rather than fill the gap from another child's guess.
  7. Educator: Has the family notification occurred, or is that still an outstanding communication task?
  8. Lead educator: I spoke with the parent after the response and recorded that contact. The notification is complete.
  9. Educator: I'll include the confirmed contact, without adding care details or diagnoses I didn't observe.
  10. Lead educator: That gives the next educator the actual sequence and avoids turning a reported event into a witnessed one.
A health update belongs in a private conversation

Fictional parent requests privacy for a record update; no actual health advice.

  1. Parent: I need to discuss a health-record update, but I don't want to talk across the doorway.
  2. Educator: We can use the private meeting space and involve Amira, our health coordinator.
  3. Parent: Thank you. Other families are arriving, and the detail isn't something I want announced.
  4. Educator: I'll simply tell Amira you have a private record question, without repeating details here.
  5. Parent: Does asking for a meeting mean the information has already been changed in the plan?
  6. Educator: No. It means we're arranging the conversation; no plan update has been confirmed.
  7. Parent: Could Amira tell me which current document we are discussing before we compare anything?
  8. Educator: I'll include that request. Comparing the actual versions will help identify what needs clarification.
  9. Parent: I have a document with me, but I don't know whether it replaces every part of the old one.
  10. Educator: Amira can review that question with you. I won't assume its date settles the whole plan.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: Childcare workers, early educators, classroom assistants, and preschool support staff
Scope: All children, families, centers, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not childcare licensing, clinical advice, developmental diagnosis, or safeguarding certification. Follow current local law, supervision requirements, individual plans, and center procedures. Do not delay urgent safeguarding or emergency action for a routine query. Materials and activities require age-appropriate risk assessment. England and US sources provide distinct contexts, not interchangeable legal rules.
Lesson 07: Health records and factual incident handovers
Original fictional case: Theo's parent, Morgan, tells educator Rosa that the allergy plan may have changed. The center still holds the earlier plan, and no revised document is available in this conversation. Health coordinator Amira is present and accepts the document query. The exercise does not authorize changing treatment, relaxing controls, or ignoring a reported safety concern.
Language workshop: A reported allergy-plan change
Communication goal: Receive a parent health-plan update and make an accountable document handoff without inventing a care decision.
Language guidance: May have changed identifies a reported uncertainty. It is neither proof that the earlier plan remains suitable nor permission to invent a replacement. Escalate promptly through the real health process so the responsible people address the discrepancy and any immediate implications.

relevant health needs. / reported change: Update described by a source but not necessarily verified. / verified revision: Updated version confirmed through the appropriate process. / current version: approval, access, and changes. / health coordinator: Person responsible for coordinating specified health-information processes. / parent report: Information attributed to a child's parent or carer. / source document: Original or authoritative record supporting an instruction or fact. / care instruction: Direction governing an aspect of care from the appropriate source. / emergency action plan: Defined arrangements for responding to an emergency. / allergen: Substance capable of triggering an allergic reaction in a susceptible person. / food intolerance: Adverse food response distinct from an immune-mediated food allergy. / allergic reaction: Immune response to an allergen that may require appropriate medical action. / anaphylaxis: Serious allergic reaction requiring immediate emergency Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Childcare and Early Education English
Professional audience: Childcare workers, early educators, classroom assistants, and preschool support staff
Scope: All children, families, centers, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not childcare licensing, clinical advice, developmental diagnosis, or safeguarding certification. Follow current local law, supervision requirements, individual plans, and center procedures. Do not delay urgent safeguarding or emergency action for a routine query. Materials and activities require age-appropriate risk assessment. England and US sources provide distinct contexts, not interchangeable legal rules.
Lesson 07: Health records and factual incident handovers
Original fictional case: Theo's parent, Morgan, tells educator Rosa that the allergy plan may have changed. The center still holds the earlier plan, and no revised document is available in this conversation. Health coordinator Amira is present and accepts the document query. No food decision, exposure, symptoms, or emergency is part of this case. Rosa must take the report seriously, preserve the difference between a reported change and a verified revision, and connect Morgan with Amira promptly. The exercise does not authorize changing treatment, relaxing controls, or ignoring a reported safety concern.
Language workshop: A reported allergy-plan change
Communication goal: Receive a parent health-plan update and make an accountable document handoff without inventing a care decision.
Language guidance: May have changed identifies a reported uncertainty. It is neither proof that the earlier plan remains suitable nor permission to invent a replacement. Escalate promptly through the real health process so the responsible people address the discrepancy and any immediate implications.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for telling me the plan may have changed. / We still hold the earlier plan. / The revised document is not available here. / I will record this as a reported possible change. / Amira has accepted the document query. / Let us connect you with Amira now. / I cannot state the new instructions without the appropriate verification. / The missing document does not mean we should ignore what you have told us. / This conversation does not establish a food or treatment decision. / We should handle the health information through the appropriate private process. / Amira's acceptance gives the query an owner. / The plan has not been confirmed as revised yet. / Do not record a staff briefing before it has occurred. / The document process should show which version is applicable. / Actual urgent concerns must follow the appropriate emergency or health route. / Possible change reported, earlier plan held, revision unavailable, query accepted by Amira.
Vocabulary: allergy action plan: Individualized document specifying management of a known allergy under appropriate guidance. / healthcare plan: Agreed information and arrangements for a person's relevant health needs. / reported change: Update described by a source but not necessarily verified. / verified revision: Updated version confirmed through the appropriate process. / current version: Document version established as applicable for use. / superseded version: Earlier document replaced through the appropriate process. / document control: Process for managing document versions, approval, access, and changes. / health coordinator: Person responsible for coordinating specified health-information processes. / parent report: Information attributed to a child's parent or carer. / source document: Original or authoritative record supporting an instruction or fact. / care instruction: Direction governing an aspect of care from the appropriate source. / emergency action plan: Defined arrangements for responding to an emergency. / allergen: Substance capable of triggering an allergic reaction in a susceptible person. / food intolerance: Adverse food response distinct from an immune-mediated food allergy. / allergic reaction: Immune response to an allergen that may require appropriate medical action. / anaphylaxis: Serious allergic reaction requiring immediate emergency response. / exposure: Contact with a substance or situation relevant to the concern. / cross-contact: Unintended transfer of an allergen from one food or surface to another. / medication authorization: Required permission or authority for medicine-related action under applicable rules. / training record: Documentation of completed relevant staff training. / staff briefing: Sharing of relevant information with staff through an appropriate process. / incident record: Factual account of a relevant event, actions, and outcomes. / handoff acknowledgment: Confirmation that the receiving person accepted the query or information. / unresolved query: Question that still requires an appropriate answer or action.
Speaking task: Read Morgan and Rosa's checked exchange. Stress may have changed, earlier plan, revision unavailable, and Amira. Switch roles. Repeat the immediate handoff without turning the parent report into new food or treatment instructions. Complete the care-plan transfer, check the key, then read it without adding symptoms, verification, or a completed staff briefing.
Partner: Morgan: Theo's parent / Rosa: Early-years educator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Parent Ellis reports that a care plan may have changed. The earlier document is held, and the revised version is unavailable. Health coordinator Noor accepts the query. No symptoms are reported.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: That distinction helps. Please keep the information private while making sure it reaches the appropriate staff through the actual process.
Rosa: Yes. Any staff briefing must be appropriate and recorded accurately. We should not claim that everyone has received new instructions before that has happened.
Morgan: Then my report is taken seriously, but no one is pretending I supplied a complete new plan today. I will speak with Amira now.
Rosa: Correct. The allergy action plan question is with Amira. We will preserve the source, the missing revision, and the actual next actions without inventing a food or treatment decision.
END REFERENCE
08Collection, confidentiality, and safeguarding communicationA collection request to verify+

Your goal

Verify a collection request respectfully, protect private information, and distinguish a stated relationship from confirmed authority.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

A collection request to verify

An unfamiliar adult, Owen, tells educator Lea that he is Nia's uncle and has come instead of the usual collector. Today's collection authority has not been verified. Lead educator Rosa is available to check the arrangement, and Nia has not been released. Owen's statement does not establish his identity, family relationship, legal status, or authority for today. Lea needs to explain the verification step calmly, maintain the actual supervision arrangements, and avoid disclosing private contact details. The scenario does not supply a completed check or permission to release Nia.

Owen: Adult requesting collection / Lea: Early-years educator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Owen state?

Answer and explanation

A. He is Nia's uncle and has come to collect her.

Owen states a relationship and purpose, but neither has been verified in this conversation.

2. What is Nia's current status?

Answer and explanation

D. She has not been released.

The brief explicitly says Nia has not been released.

3. Who is available to check the arrangement?

Answer and explanation

D. Lead educator Rosa

Rosa is the available lead educator for checking the arrangement.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Identity, relationship, and collection authority are different questions. Knowing who someone is does not automatically establish permission for this collection. A child recognizing an adult is not a substitute for the applicable authorization process. Do not infer legal status from a family title.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
collection authority
Permission established through the applicable process for someone to collect a child.verify collection authority
authorized collector
Person whose permission to collect has been confirmed as applicable.confirm the authorized collector
relationship claim
Statement about a relationship that may still require verification.distinguish a relationship claim
identity verification
Process of checking that someone is who they say they are.complete identity verification
parental responsibility
Legal responsibilities and rights whose meaning depends on the applicable jurisdiction.clarify parental responsibility
collection arrangement
Agreed details governing a child's collection.check the collection arrangement
usual collector
Person who ordinarily collects the child.contact the usual collector appropriately
substitute collector
Person proposed to collect instead of the usual person.verify a substitute collector
release
Transfer of a child into the care of an appropriately authorized person.authorize release
supervision
Active oversight appropriate to the child and setting.maintain supervision
sign-out record
Record of a child's departure and relevant collection details.complete the sign-out record
visitor check
Applicable process for checking a person entering the setting.follow the visitor check
access control
Arrangements restricting entry to authorized people or areas.maintain access control
safeguarding
Actions and arrangements to protect children from harm and promote welfare.follow safeguarding procedures
designated lead
Named person with responsibility for a specified area or process.consult the designated lead
escalation route
Defined channel for referring a concern to appropriate responsibility.use the escalation route
confidential information
Information that requires controlled access and appropriate handling.protect confidential information
disclosure
Sharing information with another person or organization.limit unnecessary disclosure
verified contact route
Communication channel confirmed as appropriate for contacting the relevant person.use a verified contact route
need-to-know basis
Limiting information access to what a person legitimately requires.share on a need-to-know basis
legal restriction
Applicable legal limit that must be checked through the proper process.verify a legal restriction
factual record
Account distinguishing observed facts, attributed statements, and unresolved matters.maintain a factual record
unverified authority
Claimed permission that has not been established through the relevant process.identify unverified authority
handover confirmation
Evidence that the authorized transfer actually occurred.record handover confirmation

Confidentiality is controlled sharing, not an unconditional promise of secrecy. Actual safeguarding duties may require information to reach appropriate people. Follow applicable local requirements and urgent procedures; this language scenario supplies neither a legal ruling nor a universal list of acceptable documents.

C Notice the language

A check is not an accusation

A calm explanation of the process can protect dignity without weakening the boundary. Unfamiliarity alone is not proof of wrongdoing, and a friendly relationship claim is not verified collection authority. Keep the child supervised and use the actual local checking and escalation arrangements.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Welcome without authorizing
Hello. Let me check the collection arrangement with our lead educator.
Acknowledge the statement
You have told me you are Nia's uncle.
Name the remaining check
Today's collection authority has not been verified yet.
Explain the boundary
We need to complete the applicable check before any release.
Name the available lead
Rosa is available to check the arrangement.
Keep supervision clear
Nia will remain under our supervision while we follow the process.
Avoid an accusation
I am explaining the required check, not accusing you of wrongdoing.
Separate identity and authority
Confirming identity is not the same as confirming permission for today.
Protect contact details
I cannot read private contact details out at the collection point.
Use the proper contact route
We will use the appropriate verified contact route.
Avoid invented legal conclusions
I cannot infer legal authority from a stated family relationship.
Keep the outcome open
The check has not produced a confirmed decision yet.
Limit the record to facts
I will record what was stated and what has actually been checked.
Avoid false release records
No handover has occurred, so we should not record one.
Keep escalation available
Any concern must follow our actual safeguarding and escalation arrangements.
Close calmly
Rosa will check the arrangement; Nia has not been released.
6 language and precision notes
You have told me
Attributes a relationship claim without certifying it.
Yet
Marks an incomplete check without promising approval.
Before any release
Makes the sequence and boundary clear.
Not the same as
Separates identity, relationship, and authority.
Private contact details
Names information that should not be casually disclosed.
Has not been released
States the actual child status rather than an anticipated outcome.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is respectful and accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Hello. Let me check today's collection arrangement with Rosa.

The opening preserves today's authorization check; recognition, identity, and a sign-out entry cannot replace it.

2. What would confirming identity alone establish?

Answer and explanation

D. Who the person is, not necessarily today's collection authority

Identity verification does not by itself establish the separate authorization or legal questions.

3. Which information should not be casually read aloud to Owen?

Answer and explanation

C. Private family contact details

The request does not authorize unnecessary disclosure of confidential family contact information.

4. Which final record fits the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Owen stated he was Nia's uncle; authority unverified; Rosa available; no release.

The factual record preserves the attributed statement and actual status without inventing approval, wrongdoing, or release.

A collection request to verify

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Owen: Adult requesting collection / Lea: Early-years educator
